Here is the SQL query: ```sql SELECT car_makers.Maker AS Maker, cars_data.Year AS Year FROM car_makers JOIN ( SELECT Id, MIN(Year) AS Year FROM cars_data GROUP BY Id ) AS earliest_years ON car_makers.Id = earliest_years.Id; ``` This query first finds the year with the minimum value from `cars_data` grouped by `Id`, which represents each unique car model, and then joins this result with the `car_makers` table using the same `Id`. SELECT To count the number of distinct store locations, you can use a SQL query that joins the 'shop' table with itself on the 'Location' column, and then counts the distinct results. Here is an example: ```sql SELECT COUNT(DISTINCT concat(T1.Location, T2.Location)) as Number_of_distinct_locations FROM shop AS T1 JOIN shop AS T2 ON T1.Location = T2.Location WHERE T1.Shop_ID <> T2.Shop_ID; ``` This query first concatenates the locations of two shops to make them unique, and then counts distinct concatenated location-pairs. By using the condition `T1.Shop_ID <> T2.Shop_ID`, we avoid counting the same location twice for each shop.

SELECT To find the template IDs that are used by more than one document, you can join the `Templates` table with the `Documents` table and use a `GROUP BY` and `HAVING` clause to filter out those with more than one document. Here's an SQL query for that: ```sql SELECT T1.Template_ID FROM Templates AS T1 JOIN Documents AS T2 ON T1.Template_Type_Code = T2.Template_Type_Code GROUP BY T1.Template_ID HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT T2.Document_ID) > 1; ``` This query groups templates by their ID and counts the number of distinct documents associated with each template. It then filters out those that have more than one document, providing you with the desired result.

The LIMIT 1 ensures that only the top result is returned.

It first calculates the average population for each district, then it counts the number of cities in each district where the population is higher than the district's average population. The result is ordered by descending district average population.

SELECT To find the names of conductors who have conducted more than one orchestra, you can use a subquery to count the unique number of orchestras each conductor has conducted and then filter out those who have conducted more than one. SELECT To find the names of the dogs for which the owner has not spent more than $1000 on treatments, you can use the SQL query as follows: ```sql SELECT name FROM Dogs JOIN ( SELECT dog_id FROM Treatments GROUP BY dog_id HAVING SUM(cost_of_treatment) <= 1000 ) AS T ON Dogs.dog_id = T.dog_id; ``` This query first finds the dogs for which the total cost of treatments is less than or equal to $1000 by grouping by `dog_id` and using an aggregate function `SUM(cost_of_treatment)`. Then, it joins this subset with the original `Dogs` table on the matching `dog_id` to find their names.

SELECT To list each charge type and its total amount, you can use a join query to combine data from the Charges table with any other tables that might have related charges (if applicable). Here's an example of how this query could look like: ```sql SELECT charge_type, SUM(charge_amount) as Total_Amount FROM Charges LEFT JOIN Treatments ON Charges.charge_id = Treatments.charge_id LEFT JOIN Dogs ON Treatments.dog_id = Dogs.dog_id GROUP BY charge_type; ``` This query assumes that charges are associated with treatments, which in turn are related to dogs. SELECT To find the most expensive charge type and its cost, you can use the following SQL query: ```sql SELECT charge_type, charge_amount FROM Charges ORDER BY charge_amount DESC LIMIT 1; ``` This query will return the name of the most expensive charge type and its corresponding cost.
SELECT To find the charge amount of the most expensive charge type, you can use the following SQL query: ```sql SELECT charge_amount FROM Charges ORDER BY charge_amount DESC LIMIT 1; ``` This query orders all charges in descending order by their amount and selects the first (i.e., the most expensive) one.

SELECT Citizenship, COUNT(*) FROM singer GROUP BY Citizenship This SQL query will return a list of different citizenships of singers and the count of singers for each citizenship. It groups all the rows in the "singer" table by their "Citizenship" column and uses COUNT(*) to count the number of occurrences in each group.
SELECT Citizenship, count(*)) FROM singer GROUP BY Citizenship; This SQL query will provide the number of singers for each citizenship. The `GROUP BY` clause groups the results by the 'Citizenship' column, and the `count(*)` function counts the number of rows in each group (i.e., the number of unique singers for each citizenship).
SELECT Citizenship FROM singer GROUP BY Citizenship ORDER BY COUNT(*) DESC LIMIT 1; This SQL query will retrieve the most common citizenship among all singers in the database. It groups the data by citizenship and orders it in descending order based on the count of each citizenship, then limits the results to the top 1 record (which should be the most common).
SELECT To find the most common singer citizenship, you can use a SQL query similar to the ones provided earlier. Here's an example: ```sql SELECT Citizenship as Most_Common_Citizenship, COUNT(*) as Count FROM singer GROUP BY Citizenship ORDER BY COUNT(*) DESC LIMIT 1; ``` This query groups the data by the `Citizenship` column of the `singer` table and orders the results in descending order based on the count. The `LIMIT 1` statement ensures that only the most common citizenship is returned.
SELECT To answer your question, you can use a SQL query that combines the `singer` and `song` tables to get the required data. Here's an example of how you could write this query: ```sql SELECT Citizenship, MAX(Net_Worth_Millions) as Max_Net_Worth FROM singer LEFT JOIN song ON singer.Singer_ID = song.Singer_ID GROUP BY Citizenship; ``` This query will join the two tables based on the `Singer_ID`, group the results by `Citizenship`, and find the maximum net worth for each citizenship. If a singer does not have any songs, they won't be included in the result because of the left join. You can adjust this query as needed to fit your specific requirements.
SELECT Citizenship, max(Net_Worth_Millions) FROM singer GROUP BY Citizenship; This SQL query will group the 'singer' table by Citizenship and find the maximum Net_Worth_Millions for each citizenship.
SELECT song.Title, singer.Name FROM song JOIN singer ON song.Singer_ID = singer.Singer_ID; This SQL query will show the titles of songs and the names of their respective singers by joining the 'song' and 'singer' tables using the shared column 'Singer_ID'.
SELECT song.Title, singer.Name FROM song INNER JOIN singer ON song.Singer_ID = singer.Singer_ID; This SQL query will return the song titles and singer names by joining the "song" table with the "singer" table based on the common column "Singer_ID". The result set would include rows with both the song title and the corresponding singer name for each row in the database.
SELECT DISTINCT Name FROM singer JOIN song ON singer.Singer_ID = song.Singer_ID WHERE song.Sales > 300000; This SQL query will return the distinct names of singers who have songs with sales more than 300,000. It joins the `singer` and `song` tables based on their foreign key relationship between the `Singer_ID`. Then it filters the results by only showing rows where the sales of the song are greater than 300,000, and finally selects only the distinct names of singers.
SELECT DISTINCT T1.Name FROM singer AS T1 JOIN song AS T2 ON T1.Singer_ID = T2.Singer_ID WHERE T2.Sales > 300000; This SQL query will return the names of singers who have sold more than 300,000 units of their songs. It does this by joining the `singer` and `song` tables on the `Singer_ID`, then filters the results to only include rows where the sales are greater than 300,000 and returns only distinct singer names using the DISTINCT keyword.
SELECT Name FROM singer JOIN song ON singer.Singer_ID = song.Singer_ID GROUP BY singer.Name HAVING COUNT(song.Song_ID) > 1 This SQL query will show the names of singers that have more than one song in the database. The query first joins the `singer` and `song` tables on the common column `Singer_ID`. It then groups the results by the singer's name, and filters out only those who have more than one song using the HAVING clause. Finally, it displays the names of the singers that meet the criteria.
SELECT Name FROM singer JOIN song ON singer.Singer_ID = song.Singer_ID GROUP BY singer.Singer_ID HAVING count(*) > 1 This SQL query will return the names of the singers who have more than one song in the database. It works by joining the singer and song tables on their common Singer_ID field, grouping by this field to count the number of songs for each singer, and then filtering out only those singers who have more than one song.
SELECT Singer.Name, sum(Song.Sales) FROM Singer JOIN Song ON Singer.Singer_ID = Song.Singer_ID GROUP BY Singer.Name This SQL query will return the names of singers and the total sales of their songs by joining the 'singer' and 'song' tables on the 'Singer_ID' field, and then grouping by 'Singer.Name'. The total sales for each singer are calculated by summing up the 'Song.Sales' column.
SELECT Name, SUM(Sales) as Total_Sales FROM song JOIN singer ON song.Singer_ID = singer.Singer_ID GROUP BY Name; This SQL query will combine the data from both tables (song and singer) based on the foreign key relationship between them. It groups the results by singer name and calculates the total sales for each singer by summing up the 'Sales' column in the 'song' table. The alias Total_Sales is given to the summed value of Sales for better readability.

SELECT To find the names of singers who do not have any songs, you can use a subquery to check if there are no corresponding rows in the `song` table for each singer. Here's how you can write the SQL query: ```sql SELECT Name FROM singer WHERE Singer_ID NOT IN (SELECT Singer_ID FROM song) ``` This query selects the names of all singers that do not have any corresponding rows in the `song` table, which means they don't have any songs.
SELECT Citizenship FROM singer WHERE Birth_Year < 1945 INTERSECT SELECT Citizenship FROM singer WHERE Birth_Year > 1955 
SELECT DISTINCT Singer.Citizenship FROM Singer JOIN ( SELECT Singer_ID FROM Singer WHERE Birth_Year < 1945 UNION SELECT Singer_ID FROM Singer WHERE Birth_Year > 1955 ) AS eligible_singers ON Singer.Singer_ID = eligible_singers.Singer_ID;
SELECT To find out how many available features are there in total, you can use a SQL query that joins the `Ref_Feature_Types`, `Other_Available_Features`, and `Other_Property_Features` tables. Here is an example of how you could write this query: ```sql SELECT COUNT(DISTINCT oaf.feature_id) FROM Other_Available_Features oaf JOIN Ref_Feature_Types rft ON oaf.feature_type_code = rft.feature_type_code; ``` This query joins the `Other_Available_Features` table with the `Ref_Feature_Types` table on the `feature_type_code` column, and then counts the distinct `feature_id` values from the resulting join, which should give you the total number of available features.
